For this purpose note that a first-order approximation to the evolved state can be obtained by solving each problem

-\mathrm{i}\frac{d}{dt}\ket{\psi(t)}=\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\ket{\psi(t)}

independently.
10
10

10

The prefactors
\frac{1}{L\|\ket{\psi}\|}
are absorbed into the normalization of the site tensor that is currently evolved.

Let
\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}
be the solution of the
j
-th problem.
An approximation to the overall time-evolved state from
t\rightarrow t+\delta
can then be obtained by sequentially solving the initial value problems (setting

\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{0}}(t)}\equiv\ket{\psi(t)}
)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\frac{d}{dt}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)}

(161)

and identifying
\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}\equiv\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{L}}(t+\delta)}
with the approximated time evolved state.
Comparing the formal Taylor expansions of the exactly integrated state
\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}_{\mathrm{exact}}
with the approximation one readily finds

\displaystyle\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}_{\mathrm{exact}}

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi(t)}-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}\ket{\psi(t)}-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\hat{H}^{2}\ket{\psi(t)}+\cdots

(162)

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t+\delta)}

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}+\delta\frac{d}{dt}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}+\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}+\cdots

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)}-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}+\cdots

(163)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi(t)}-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}\ket{\psi(t)}-\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i\leq j}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}}\ket{\psi(t)}\cdots

(164)

where we have already applied
Eq.

161
to the expansion of the partially time-evolved intermediate state
\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t+\delta)}
around
\delta=0
.
Replacing
\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)}
by its Taylor expansion around
\delta=0
generates a recursion for the partially time-evolved states.
Applying this recursion
L
times and using the initial condition
\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{0}}(t)}=\ket{\psi(t)}
yields the last equation.
As expected,
\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{L}}(t+\delta)}
coincides with
Eq.

160
up to the first order of the expansion.
The first error occurs at second-order terms in the expansion and is given by

\displaystyle\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}_{\mathrm{exact}}-\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}=\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i<j}\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]\ket{\psi(t)}

(165)

and further commutators at arbitrary high orders.
Due to the dependence of the reduced problem currently solved onto the previous solution in
Eq.

161
the projectors
\hat{P}^{L,\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}
in our integration scheme are in fact time-dependent because they need to be constructed from site tensors
A_{j^{\prime}<j}(t+\delta)
that have already been evolved. In contrast,
\hat{P}^{R,\ket{\psi}}_{j+1}
is constructed from unevolved site tensors
B_{j^{\prime}>j}(t)
.
We hence have to actually solve the
L
initial value problems

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\frac{d}{dt}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\left[\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}(t,t+\delta)\hat{H}\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}(t,t+\delta)\right]\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)}

(166)

with
\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}(t,t+\delta)\equiv\hat{P}^{L,\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)}}_{j-1}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}_{j}\otimes\hat{P}^{R,\ket{\psi(t)}}_{j+1}
.
Note that while these problems appear to evolve over
L
time steps with step size
\delta
, they only do so using different local Hamiltonians.
Those local problems are then connected via their initial conditions (c.f.
Eq.

161
) to generate a global time evolution by
\delta
under (an approximation of) the global Hamiltonian.

Having decoupled the global Schrödinger equation into
L
local problems, we can take advantage of the local representation of the state by multiplying each local differential equation at site
j
with the bond maps
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R}_{j+1}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\frac{d}{dt}\left[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R}_{j+1}\right]\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}}

\displaystyle=\left[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R}_{j+1}\right]\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow-\mathrm{i}\frac{d}{dt}M_{j}

\displaystyle=H^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}M_{j}

(167)

where we defined effective reduced-site state and operator representations
M_{j}
and
H^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}
, respectively (see also
Figs.

12
and
13
).
It can be checked easily that the projection of the effective problems onto their reduced site spaces at site
j
leaves the solution invariant.
Carrying out the differentiation on the left-hand site explicitly yields a sum of partial differentiations.
However, on the right hand site
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}
acts only on the reduced site spaces and hence all differentials must vanish except the one acting on the tensor in the reduced site space.

Unfortunately, a direct adoption of the recursive solution strategy proposed above is not possible because the current problem at site
j
requires the projectors to be constructed from left-/right-canonical site-tensors
A_{1},\ldots,A_{j-1},B_{j+1},\ldots,B_{L}
.
However, the solution
M_{j-1}
of the previous reduced problem is not in general in a canonical representation so that in order to construct the next projector
\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}
we need to perform a basis transformation first.
There is no prescription in the derived decomposition scheme which corresponds to such a basis transformation keeping the evolved site tensors in canonical form.
On the other hand we can of course rewrite the updated site tensor as
M_{j-1}\rightarrow A_{j-1}R_{\underline{j-1}}
in a left-canonical representation.
But, as already pointed out, we have to discard the transformation matrix
R_{\underline{j-1}}
to obey the decomposition scheme.
To proceed with the next reduced problem, we need to resolve the situation of having
2
different basis representations between the sites
(j-1,j)
.
Hence, we introduce a transformation between the unevolved and evolved site tensors
A_{j-1},M_{j}
.
The simplest guess is to consider a direct mapping between the unevolved and evolved site tensor basis sets.
This transformation can be readily obtained from the iterative solution strategy and is performed by projecting the evolved onto the unevolved bond space.
To see this we write the partially evolved state explicitely in the mixed canonical form

\displaystyle\ket{\psi(t,t+\delta)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{L},\\
m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{A^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}}_{1}(t+\delta)\cdots A^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}_{j-1}(t+\delta)}_{\equiv\psi^{L;\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j-1}}_{j-1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)}M^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}_{j;m_{j-1},m_{j}}(t)\underbrace{B^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}}_{j+1}(t)\cdots B^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{L}}_{L}(t)}_{\equiv\psi^{R;\sigma_{j+1},\ldots,\sigma_{L}}_{j+1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}(t)}\ket{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}\cdots\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{L}}

(168)

and introduce a compact notation for the left/right partition’s basis states

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{L}_{j}(t)}_{m_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j}}\psi^{L;\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j}}_{j;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}(t)\ket{\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{j}}

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{R}_{j}(t)}_{m_{j-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{j},\ldots,\sigma_{L}}\psi^{R;\sigma_{j},\ldots,\sigma_{L}}_{j;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}(t)\ket{\sigma_{j}\cdots\sigma_{L}}\;.

(169)

Without truncation
\ket{\psi^{L}_{j}(t)}_{m_{j}}
and
\ket{\psi^{R}_{j}(t)}_{m_{j-1}}
would constitute complete maps from the physical degrees of freedom in the left/right partition and we could perform an exact basis transformation mapping the evolved into the unevolved left basis

\displaystyle\ket{\psi(t)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{j-1}}\ket{\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t)}_{m_{j-1}}{\vphantom{\ket{\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t)}}}{}_{m_{j-1}}\hskip-3.99994pt\braket{\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t)|\psi(t,t+\delta)}

(170)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{\prime}_{j-1},\\
\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\end{subarray}}\ket{\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t)}_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\underbrace{{\vphantom{\ket{\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t)}}}{}_{m_{j-1}}\hskip-3.99994pt\braket{\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t)\lvert\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t+\delta)}_{m^{\prime}_{j-1}}}_{Q_{\underline{j-1};m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{\prime}_{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)}M^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}_{j;m^{\prime}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}(t)\ket{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\ket{\psi^{R}_{j}(t)}_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}

(171)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{L},\\
m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\\
m^{\prime}_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\psi^{L;\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1}}_{j-1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}(t)Q_{\underline{j-1};m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{\prime}_{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)M^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}_{j;m^{\prime}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}(t)\psi^{R}_{j+1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}(t)\ket{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}\cdots\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{L}}\;.

(172)

and the matrix elements
Q_{\underline{j-1};m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{\prime}_{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)
of the basis transformation
Q_{\underline{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)
are constructed from

\displaystyle Q_{\underline{j-1};m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{\prime}_{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L;\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}_{j-1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}(t)\psi^{L;\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}_{j-1;m^{\prime}_{j-1}}(t+\delta)\;.

(173)

The transformation
Q_{\underline{j}}(t,t+\delta)
maps bond basis states
\ket{\psi^{L}_{j}(t+\delta)}_{m_{j}}
which are optimized to represent the evolved state
\ket{\psi(t+\delta)}
into bond basis states
\ket{\psi^{L}_{j}(t)}_{m_{j}}
which are optimized to represent the unevolved state
\ket{\psi(t)}
.
Now, if we would let act
Q_{\underline{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)
to the already optimized canonical site tensor
A^{\sigma_{j-1}}_{j-1}(t+\delta)
then the effect would be to undo the previous site optimization.
Hence, the inverse transformation
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\underline{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)
is employed to transform the unevolved bond basis labeled by
m_{j-1}
of the current site tensor
M_{j}(t)
.
Observing that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\underline{j-1}}
is constructed from the above introduced bond maps
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L}_{j-1}(t)
and
\psi^{L}_{j-1}(t+\delta)
which themselves should be build recursively, the transformation can be updated and applied before solving the
j
-th problem via

\displaystyle Q_{\underline{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j-1}(t)Q_{\underline{j-2}}(t,t+\delta)A_{j-1}(t+\delta),\quad M_{j}(t)\longrightarrow\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\underline{j-1}}(t,t+\delta)M_{j}(t)\;.

(174)

If we allow for truncation the error incurred by this mapping depends on the overlap
\braket{\psi(t+\delta)|\psi(t)}
as well as the discarded weight.
This basis transformation is mostly motivated by its straightforward availability during the sweeping procedure.
However, to the best of our knowledge there is no mathematical justification and we can only give the physical motivation that for small time steps
\delta
the time-evolved state is expected to be relatively close to the unevolved state (deviation
\propto L\delta^{2}
as follows from the consideration in
Section

6.1.1
).

Instead of mapping onto the space of a single site, in practice we map
onto the space of two sites. The two-site local TDSE is solved using
the time-dependent Lanczos approach to obtain
A_{j}(t+\delta)
. The
original orthogonality center MPS tensor
M_{j+1}(t)
is then
projected onto the new left basis as described above. This allows for
a flexible adaptation of not only the tensor
A_{j}
itself but also
of the bond basis and – if necessary – MPS bond dimension between
sites
j
and
j+1
.

Historically, only this two-site variant was used; but in analogy to
the 2TDVP method presented later, it may well make sense to initially
use the two-site local Krylov method until the desired maximal bond
dimension has been obtained and then switch to the single-site
integrator to save computational effort.

6.1.1
Errors

Four errors are present in the local Krylov method when used in its (standard) two-site variant. The smallest of those stems from the inexact solution of the local TDSE
Eq.

167
. This error can be made very small using a precise solver; in practice, a Krylov exponential as described in
Section

5
with very few (4-5) vectors is sufficient. The second error is the standard truncation error incurred during the SVD to split the merged two-site tensors again while truncating to the desired bond dimension. This error can be measured and observed throughout the calculation and is much the same as in the other methods.

The third error is due to the approximation in

Eq.

157
. This projection error is
difficult to measure and strongly depends on the initial state. If the
initial state has a reasonably large bond dimension and the
Hamiltonian has reasonably short-range interactions, this error will
be very small. The longer the interactions in the Hamiltonian, the
larger the state has to be. In the two-site method, nearest-neighbor
interactions can be handled at all bond dimensions, in the single-site
variant, only on-site interactions are error-free at small bond
dimensions. The projection error is in particular problematic when
globally quenching from a product state with a very non-local
Hamiltonian (e.g. resulting from a 2D
\to
1D map). When calculating
equilibrium Green’s functions for short-range Hamiltonians, this error
is quite negligible.

Finally, there is an error due to the sequential solution of the local
TDSE as resulting from the Lie-Trotter decomposition. This error

can
be quantified, but doing so requires some additional work
which will follow now: We continue from the Taylor expansion

Eq.

164
. We emphasize that the action of the commutators

\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]\ket{\psi(t_{0})}

need to be evaluated with respect to the iteration the commutators are generated
from.

Figure 16
:

Evaluation of
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{2}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}(t)}
at the example of a four-site system. The tensors
L_{i}/R_{i}
correspond to partially contracted MPS-MPO-MPS-networks.
The burgundy-shaded rectangular area at the top encloses the reduced site tensor
\psi^{C;\sigma_{i},\ldots,\sigma_{j}}_{i,\ldots,j;m_{i-1},m_{j}}
.
The commutator
\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{2}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}\right]\ket{\psi^{\Pi}_{3}(t)}
requires also the calculation of
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{2}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}(t)}
which is obtained from vertically flipping the tensors covered by the central rectangular grey area between the sites
(i,j)
, i.e.,
(2,3)
in the presented example.

Consider, for instance, the action of
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{2}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}\ket{\psi(t)}
, which is generated from the first-order contribution
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}}
and subsequent application of
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{2}}
. Thus the commutator also needs to be evaluated considering the partial solution
\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}}
, so that in general we have for
i<j

\displaystyle\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]\ket{\psi(t)}

\displaystyle=\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}\;.

(175)

In
Fig.

16
the action of
\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}
is demonstrated in case of a four-site system with
i=2
,
j=3
by performing most of the contractions graphically.
In order to obtain the matrix element with an arbitrary state
\ket{\phi}
we will introduce a compact notation for contractions of MPS and MPO site-tensors with the boundary tensors (partially contracted MPS-MPO-MPS-networks
L_{j}/R_{j}
)

\displaystyle L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}

\displaystyle\equiv L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}M^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}=\sum_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\left(\sum_{w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\left(\sum_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}L^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}_{j-1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\right)W^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}_{j,w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\right)M^{\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}_{j,m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}

(176)

\displaystyle E_{j}R_{j+1}

\displaystyle\equiv\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}M^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}=\sum_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\left(\sum_{w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}W^{\sigma^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}_{j;w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\left(\sum_{m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}M^{\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}_{j;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}R^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},w^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}_{j+1;m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}\right)\right)

(177)

with the transfer tensors
E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}M^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}
.
We also need transfer tensors with the target state which we define by
E^{\phi}_{j}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{j}W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}M^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}
.
Finally, there will be
open
bonds at sites
i
and
j
that correspond to the contractions originating from the “brace”-contractions in the projectors
\hat{P}^{L,\ket{\psi}}_{i}
and
\hat{P}^{R,\ket{\psi}}_{i}
as well as
\hat{P}^{L,\ket{\psi}}_{j+1}
and
\hat{P}^{R,\ket{\psi}}_{j+1}
, and we will label these bonds explicitly.
Considering for instance the first summand of the commutator at
i=2,j=3
, that is
\braket{\phi|\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{2}}\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}|\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{3}}(t)}
(c.f.
Fig.

16
) the left part of the contractions can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}\otimes\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}E^{\phi}_{2}\right)_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}\delta_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{1}\right)\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}\otimes E^{\phi}_{2}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}\delta_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{2}}\;.

(178)

To obtain a compact notation for the right part we introduce the “dangling” transfer tensors
D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}=W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}M^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}
if only the ket site-tensor of the state is included and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muM\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}
if only the bra site-tensor is considered.
In the same manner as for the left part we can now write for the right contractions compactly

\displaystyle\sum_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}\left(D_{3}R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{4}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}\otimes\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{3}R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{4}\right)_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}\delta_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}\left(D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{3}\right)\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{4}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{4}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}\delta_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{3},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{3}}\;.

(179)

In general we obtain for the matrix element of the commutator with the target state
\ket{\phi}
(suppressing the Kronecker-
\delta
for brevity)

\displaystyle\braket{\phi|\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{i}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]|\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\\
m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\end{subarray}}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1}\right)\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i}\cdots E_{j-1}\otimes E^{\phi}_{i}\cdots E^{\phi}_{j-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\left(D_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{j}\right)\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}+\cdots

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1}\\
m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i}\end{subarray}}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i-1}}\left(D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{i}\right)\left(E_{i+1}\cdots E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes E^{\phi}_{i+1}\cdots E^{\phi}_{j}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{i}}\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\right)\;.

(180)

It becomes immediately clear that only those terms in the Hamiltonian contribute to the commutator which cross the bond
(j-1,j)
or
(i,i+1)
.
For the current purpose it suffices to find a general estimate for the incurred error
\propto\delta^{2}
in the Taylor expansion.
Thus, we will only consider the contributions from nearest-neighbor interactions and hence set
i=j-1
so that

\displaystyle\braket{\phi|\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]|\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\\
m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\end{subarray}}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\right)\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\otimes E^{\phi}_{j-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\left(D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{j}\right)\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}+\cdots

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\\
m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}}\left(D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{j-1}\right)\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes E^{\phi}_{j}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\right)\;.

(181)

The crucial observation here is that for each “open” bond index pair we can treat the combined MPS-MPS and MPO-MPO tensor-contractions over the bond
(j-1,j)
as scalar product between the left and right part of the system.
We can thus write for the first summand for each open index pair

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\right)\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\otimes E^{\phi}_{j-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}}_{\equiv\bra{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}}\underbrace{\vphantom{\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\right)\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\otimes E^{\phi}_{j-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}}\left(D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{j}\right)\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}}}_{\equiv\ket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lvert\ket{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}+\ket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}-\braket{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}|e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}-\braket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}|d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right)

(182)

and for the second summand

\displaystyle\underbrace{\vphantom{\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes E^{\phi}_{j}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\right)}\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}}\left(D^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phi}_{j-1}\right)}_{\equiv\bra{d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}}\underbrace{\left(E^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes E^{\phi}_{j}\right)_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1},m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}}\left(R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\otimes R^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j+1}\right)}_{\equiv\ket{e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lvert\ket{d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}-\ket{e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}-\braket{d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}|d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}-\braket{e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}|e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right)\;.

(183)

We can bound the sums over the open bonds for the scalar products in terms of absolute values of expectation values of effective Hamiltonians
\hat{h}^{L/R,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}
at site
j

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\braket{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}|e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\ket{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\hat{h}^{L,\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\psi^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\cdot\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\hat{h}^{L,\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\psi^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\right\rvert

(184)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\end{subarray}}\braket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}|d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\ket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\left[\hat{h}^{R,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\right]^{2}\psi^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\right\rvert

(185)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\end{subarray}}\braket{d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}|d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-2}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\ket{d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\left[\hat{h}^{L,\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\right]^{2}\psi^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1}\right\rvert

(186)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\braket{e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}|e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\ket{e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\hat{h}^{R,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\psi^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\cdot\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\phi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\hat{h}^{R,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\psi^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\right\rvert

(187)

where we defined

\hat{h}^{L,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}=L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\left[\hat{h}^{L,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}\right]^{2}=\left(L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\otimes L^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j-1}\right)\left(W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\otimes W^{{\phantom{\prime}}}_{j}\right)

(188)

and in a similiar way
\hat{h}^{R,\mathrm{eff}}_{j}
replacing
L_{j-1}\rightarrow R_{j+1}
.
The formal absolute values “
\lvert\cdot\rvert
” on the right side can be estimated by replacing the
L_{j}/R_{j}
tensors with fractions of the overall energy expectation value.
This is a valid approximation as long as there are no interactions connecting the left/right contracted MPS-MPO-MPS tensor networks with sites to the right/left of them which was exactly the condition for non-vanishing contributions to the commutator.
We hence compare only squares of single-site expectation values with either one or two MPO site tensors sandwiched between the effective site tensors at sites
j-1,j
.
For a large system with smoothly varying site tensors the differences at neighboring sites are negligible so that the commutator can be estimated to

\displaystyle\braket{\phi|\left[\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j-1}},\hat{H}^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}\right]|\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lvert\ket{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}+\ket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\ket{d^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}-\ket{e^{R}_{j}\otimes e^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lvert\ket{e^{L}_{j-1}\otimes e^{L,\phi}_{j-1}}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\ket{d^{R}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R,\phi}_{j}}\right\rvert^{2}\right)\;.

(189)

The last expression can be estimated easily by the coupling strength of the nearest-neighbor interaction term which we denote by
\Gamma
.
The contributions from the boundary tensors
L_{j}/R_{j}
are bounded by the square of the system size and cancel with the prefactors
\nicefrac{{1}}{{L}}
of each projected Hamiltonian.
We hence conclude this analysis by the somewhat straightforward statement that in general the error at second order in
\delta
scales with
L\cdot\Gamma^{2}
.
However, there is one special situation which simplifies the preceeding arguments drastically namely if we set
\ket{\phi}=\ket{\psi^{\hat{\Pi}^{\ket{\psi}}_{j}}(t)}
.
Then the commutator compares only local overlaps between site tensors sandwiched between either one or two MPO tensors on neighboring sites which scales as
\nicefrac{{1}}{{L}}
.
Therefore equal-time observables are evolved with very high precision
\sim\nicefrac{{1}}{{L^{2}}}
and the contribution due to nearest-neighbor interactions is strongly suppressed.

6.1.2
Algorithm

Algorithm 3

Common helper
functions for the local Krylov method and the TDVP algorithms.

1:

procedure

Contract-Left
(
L_{j-1}
,
W_{j}
,
A_{j}
)
\triangleright
left-contraction as in DMRG

2:

return

L_{j;m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}=\sum_{\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1}}L_{j-1;m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1}}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}W_{j;w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}^{\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}A^{\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}_{j;m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}

3:

end

procedure

4:

procedure

Contract-Right
(
R_{j+1}
,
W_{j}
,
B_{j}
)
\triangleright
right contraction as in DMRG

5:

return

R_{j;m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1}}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1}}=\sum_{\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}W_{j;w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}^{\sigma^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}B^{\sigma^{\prime}_{j}}_{j;m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j-1},m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}R_{j+1;m^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j},w^{\phantom{\prime}}_{j}}

6:

end

procedure

7:

procedure

Initialize
(MPO
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
, MPS
\{M_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
)

8:

L_{0;m_{0}}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0},w_{0}}\leftarrow 1
and
R_{L+1;m_{L}}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{L},w_{L}}\leftarrow 1

9:

Right-normalize
\{M_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}\to\{B_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
from right to left

10:

for

j\in[L,2]

do

11:

R_{j}\leftarrow\textsc{Contract-Right}(R_{j+1},W_{j},B_{j})

12:

end

for

13:

return

L_{0}
,
\{R_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L+1}
,
\{B_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}

14:

end

procedure

15:

procedure

Timestep
(
\delta
,
L_{0}
,
\{R_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L+1}
,
\{W_{j}\}
,
\{M_{1},B_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L}
)

16:

\{L_{j}\}_{j=0}^{L-1}
,
\{A_{j},M_{L}\}_{j=1}^{L-1}

\leftarrow

Sweep-Right
(
\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}
,
L_{0}
,
\{R_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L}
,
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
,
\{M_{1},B_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L}
)

17:

\{R_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L+1}
,
\{M_{1},B_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L}

\leftarrow

Sweep-Left
(
\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}
,
R_{L+1}
,
\{L_{j}\}_{j=0}^{L-1}
,
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
,
\{A_{j},M_{L}\}_{j=1}^{L-1}
)

end

Algorithm 4

The local 2-site Krylov
method. Also cf.
Algorithm

3
for
definitions of
Initialize
,
Contract-Left
,

Contract-Right
and the needed overall
Timestep

method.

1:

procedure

Sweep-Right
(
\delta
,
L_{0}
,
\{R_{j}\}_{j=3}^{L+1}
,
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
,
\{M_{1},B_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L}
)

2:

A_{1},C_{\underline{1}}\leftarrow M_{1}
via QR;
M_{2}\leftarrow C_{\underline{1}}\cdot B_{2}

3:

P^{\sigma_{1}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0},m_{1}}\leftarrow A^{\sigma_{1}}_{1;m_{0},m_{1}}

\triangleright
entry-for-entry copy, only label of
m_{0}
adapted

4:

for

j\in[1,L-1]

do

5:

T_{j,j+1}\leftarrow\sum_{m_{j}}A_{j;m_{j-1},m_{j}}^{\sigma_{j}}M_{j+1;m_{j},m_{j+1}}^{\sigma_{j+1}}

6:

T_{j,j+1}\leftarrow\mathrm{exp}(-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j,j+1)})T_{j,j+1}
using
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j,j+1)}\equiv L_{j-1}\cdot W_{j}\cdot W_{j+1}\cdot R_{j+2}

7:

if

j\neq L-1

then

8:

A^{\prime}_{j}\leftarrow T_{j,j+1}
via SVD and truncation,
S
and
V
discarded

9:

L_{j}\leftarrow\textsc{Contract-Left}(L_{j-1},W_{j},A^{\prime}_{j})
;  delete
R_{j+2}

10:

P^{\prime\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}_{m_{j}}\leftarrow\sum_{\sigma_{j}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j-1}}P^{\sigma_{j}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j-1},m_{j}}\cdot\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\prime\sigma_{j}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}

\triangleright
complete projector

11:

M^{\sigma_{j+1}}_{j+1;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j},m_{j+1}}\leftarrow\sum_{m_{j}}P^{\prime\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}_{m_{j}}\cdot M^{\sigma_{j+1}}_{j+1;m_{j},m_{j+1}}

\triangleright
project
M_{j+1}
into new left basis

12:

A_{j+1},C_{\underline{j+1}}\leftarrow M_{j+1}
via QR;
M_{j+2}\leftarrow C_{\underline{j+1}}\cdot B_{j+2}

13:

P^{\sigma_{j+1}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j},m_{j+1}}\leftarrow\sum_{m_{j}}P^{\prime\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}_{m_{j}}\cdot A^{\sigma_{j}}_{j+1;m_{j},m_{j+1}}

14:

else

15:

A^{\prime}_{j},C_{\underline{j}},B_{j+1}\leftarrow T_{j,j+1}
via SVD and truncation

16:

M_{j+1}\leftarrow C_{\underline{j}}\cdot B_{j+1}

17:

end

if

18:

A_{j}\leftarrow A^{\prime}_{j}

19:

end

for

20:

return

\{L_{j}\}_{j=0}^{L-2}
,
\{A_{j},M_{L}\}_{j=1}^{L-1}

21:

end

procedure

22:

procedure

Sweep-Left
(
\delta
,
R_{L+1}
,
\{L_{j}\}_{j=0}^{L-2}
,
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
,
\{A_{j},M_{L}\}_{j=1}^{L-1}
)

B_{L},C_{\underline{L-1}}\leftarrow M_{L}
via QR;
M_{L-1}\leftarrow A_{L-1}\cdot C_{\underline{L-1}}

24:

P^{\sigma_{L}}_{m_{L-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{L}}\leftarrow B^{\sigma_{L}}_{L;m_{L-1},m_{L}}

\triangleright
entry-for-entry copy, only label of
m_{L}
adapted

for

j\in[L,2]

do

27:

T_{j-1,j}\leftarrow\mathrm{exp}(-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j-1,j)})T_{j-1,j}
using
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j-1,j)}\equiv L_{j-2}\cdot W_{j-1}\cdot W_{j}\cdot R_{j+1}

if

then

via SVD and truncation,
U
and

;  delete
L_{j-2}

P^{\prime\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j-1}}_{m_{j-1}}\leftarrow\sum_{\sigma_{j}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}P^{\sigma_{j}}_{m_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\prime\sigma_{j}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j-1},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mum\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}

\triangleright

procedure

The two-site local Krylov method is described in detail in

Algorithm

4
. It relies on some basic initialization
functions familiar from the DMRG algorithm which are summarized in

Algorithm

3
.

6.2
The time-dependent variational principle (TDVP)

There is an alternative to the Lie-Trotter decomposition introduced in
the previous
Section

6.1
which also results in a series of
local problems: the time-dependent variational
principle
[
36
,
37
]
. The motivation of this approach is quite
different: its primary aim is to constrain the time evolution to a
specific manifold of matrix-product states of a given initial bond
dimension. To do so, it projects the action of the Hamiltonian into
the tangent space to this manifold and then solves the TDSE solely
within the manifold. While ideally used in its single-site variant,
the two-site variant allows for flexibility in the bond dimension.

Figure 17
:
Right-hand side of the effective
single-site forwards-evolving Schrödinger equation (with
j=4
). The effective
Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}
is given by the cornered
green, orange and red tensors. The effective state is given by the
blue circled tensor
M_{j}
. During the calculation, the connected
dashed lines are contracted, resulting in a new tensor with three
legs (the three open dashed lines).

Figure 18
:
Right-hand side of the
effective center matrix backward-evolving Schrödinger
equation with
\underline{j}=4
. The effective state over the bond between sites
j
and

j+1
is given by the grey diamond
C_{\underline{j}}
. During the
calculation, the connected dashed lines are contracted, resulting
in a new tensor with two legs (the two open dashed
lines).

6.2.1
Derivation

The main difference between the TDVP and the local Krylov method is in
the derivation of the series of these local time-dependent Schrödinger
equations and the recovery of the original-time step after each local
forward update: Instead of simply projecting the original site tensor
onto the new basis as done in the local Krylov approach, the TDVP
explicitly solves a backwards-evolution equation.
To embark on the derivation for the TDVP, we need to introduce a few
additional ingredients: First, we define the
single-site tangent
space

T_{|\psi\rangle}
of a given MPS
|\psi\rangle
as the space
spanned by variations of single MPS tensors. One may e.g. change the
first site tensor of the MPS keeping all others fixed to obtain a new
state and combine the result with another MPS where only the second
site tensor was changed, but one may not change two (or more) site
tensors in the same basis state. The projector

\hat{P}_{T_{\ket{\psi}}}
which projects onto this tangent space is
given by
[
36
,
37
]

\hat{P}_{T_{|\psi\rangle}}=\sum_{j=1}^{L}\hat{P}_{j-1}^{L,|\psi\rangle}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}^{\vphantom{L\ket{\psi}}}_{j}\otimes\hat{P}_{j+1}^{R,|\psi\rangle}-\sum_{j=1}^{L-1}\hat{P}_{j}^{L,|\psi\rangle}\otimes\hat{P}_{j+1}^{R,|\psi\rangle}\quad,

(190)

where
\hat{P}^{L,\ket{\psi}}_{j}
(
\hat{P}^{R,\ket{\psi}}_{j}
) projects on the sites left (right)
of and including site
n
(cf.
Fig.

14
) and is
exactly the same as the projectors used in the local Krylov method.
As before, these projectors use the gauge-fixed left- and
right-normalised MPS tensors, i.e. they depend on the MPS

|\psi\rangle
and can be written as

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{L,\ket{\psi}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{j}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}}_{j;m_{j}}\otimes\psi^{L;\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j}}_{j;m_{j}}

(191)

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{R,\ket{\psi}}_{j;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{L},\sigma_{j},\ldots,\sigma_{L}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{j-1}}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R;\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j},\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\sigma\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{L}}_{j;m_{j-1}}\otimes\psi^{R;\sigma_{j},\ldots,\sigma_{L}}_{j;m_{j-1}}

(192)

where
\psi^{L(R)}_{j}
is the collection of left- (right-) normalised
MPS tensors on site
j
and to its left (right) as in the local Krylov
approach. The first contributing sum in
Eq.

190

filters for all MPS which differ at most on one site from

|\psi\rangle
, whereas the second contributing sum removes all those
states which coincide with
|\psi\rangle
. Put differently,
individual tangent vectors are constructed by replacing any
orthogonality center tensor
M_{j}
of the MPS by another tensor
N_{j}

which is orthogonal to
M_{j}
, i.e.,
M_{j}\cdot\bar{N}_{j}=0
. In
contrast to the projectors
\hat{\Pi}_{j}^{\ket{\psi}}
of the local
Krylov method, the total projector
\hat{P}_{T_{\ket{\psi}}}
projects
onto some subspace of the Hilbert space and is only coincidentally
written as a sum of local terms.

Second, when inserting the projector
\hat{P}_{T_{|\psi\rangle}}
into
the TDSE, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|\psi\rangle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\hat{P}_{T_{|\psi\rangle}}\hat{H}|\psi\rangle

(193)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{L}\hat{P}_{j-1}^{L,|\psi\rangle}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}^{\vphantom{L\ket{\psi}}}_{j}\otimes\hat{P}_{j+1}^{R,|\psi\rangle}\hat{H}|\psi\rangle+\mathrm{i}\sum_{j=1}^{L-1}\hat{P}_{j}^{L,|\psi\rangle}\otimes\hat{P}_{j+1}^{R,|\psi\rangle}\hat{H}|\psi\rangle\;.

(194)

While an exact solution of
Eq.

194
is still not
possible, we can approximate it by solving each term individually and
sequentially, i.e., solve
L
forward-evolving equations of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\hat{P}_{j-1}^{L,\ket{\psi}}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}^{\vphantom{L\ket{\psi}}}_{j}\otimes\hat{P}_{j+1}^{R,\ket{\psi}}\hat{H}|\psi\rangle

(195)

and
L-1
backward-evolving equations of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=+\mathrm{i}\hat{P}_{j}^{L,\ket{\psi}}\otimes\hat{P}_{j+1}^{R,\ket{\psi}}\hat{H}|\psi\rangle\;.

(196)

We then multiply each individual equation above by the single-site map

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L}_{j-1}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R}_{j+1}
or the
center-bond map
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{L}_{j}\otimes\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{R}_{j+1}
,
respectively. As a result, instead of having to work with the full
MPS
|\psi\rangle
, we can work with the effective single-site and
effective center matrix tensors and associated local Schrödinger
equations directly:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}M^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j}M^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j}

(197)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}C^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{\underline{j}}

\displaystyle=+\mathrm{i}\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\underline{j}}C^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{\underline{j}}\quad.

(198)

The tensor contraction in the RHS of
Eq.

197
is
graphically represented in
Fig.

17
, while the RHS of

Eq.

198
is shown in
Fig.

18
.
Each of these equations can be solved with a local application of the
Krylov method much as in DMRG or the local Krylov method of the
previous section.

Sweeping right-to-left (rather than left-to-right) through the system
results in solving the equations in reverse order. This turns the
initial first-order integrator into a second-order integrator,
reducing the time step error (as described in
Section

6.2.2
)
from
O(\delta)
to
O(\delta^{2})
if both sweeps are done with halved
time steps
\delta/2
.

An interesting property of the single-site TDVP variant (1TDVP) is
that the projection of the Hamiltonian onto the MPS manifold occurs
before the time evolution, the projection is the
only
step
necessary to obtain the Lie-Trotter decomposition of the Hamiltonian,
and no truncation has to happen after the evolution. As such, both
the norm and energy of the state are conserved under real-time
evolution. This is in contrast to the local Krylov method, where the
basis transformation generated by the
Q
tensors (as described at the
end of
Section

6.1
) is
not
part of the projector
and hence introduces an additional error.
Alternatively, it is
straightforward to extend the mechanism to a two-site variant. This
2TDVP forward-evolves a local tensor
M_{(j,j+1)}
which needs to be
split into two separate site tensors again following the evolution.
The advantage is that the bond dimension of the state can be adapted
on the fly. However, norm and energy are now no longer conserved
exactly if a truncation of the evolved bond is necessary.

6.2.2
Errors

The TDVP has four sources of errors:
firstly, there is a
projection error
due to the projection of
the full time-dependent Schrödinger equation (TDSE) onto the MPS
manifold of limited bond dimension. This error is particularly severe
if the MPS in question has a small bond dimension, but it is exactly
zero if the MPS has maximal (exponentially growing) bond dimension.
However, the projection error occurs during the projection of the TDSE
onto the relevant subspace, i.e.,
before
the time evolution. As
such, it cannot lead to a violation of energy conservation or change
the norm of the time-evolved state (during real-time evolution).
Using a two- or multi-site variance
[
86
]
it is
possible to estimate this projection error. If the
n
-site variance
of the state is large, the
(n-1)
TDVP will provide inadequate
results. Vice versa, if the up-to-
n
-site variance of a state is
small, the
n
TDVP will consider this state an eigenstate of the
Hamiltonian and the time evolution will only add a global phase to the
state. As a corollary, the 2TDVP can evolve Hamiltonians with only
nearest-neighbor interactions without incurring a projection error.

Second, the chain of forwards and backwards evolutions can be
considered a sequential solution of a series of coupled TDSE (which
are the result of the projection above), each describing the evolution
of any particular site tensor. Except in the special case that all
these evolutions describe exactly the same dynamics (due to the state
having maximal bond dimension), there is a finite time-step error of
order
O(\delta^{3})
per time step and order
O(\delta^{2})
per unit
time. In practice, the prefactor of this error is often much smaller
than, e.g., in a TEBD calculation, in particular if the bond dimension
of the input state is reasonably large. If the bond dimension is very
small, the time-step error will be relatively large.

Third, the 2TDVP contains a SVD to split the evolved two-site tensor
into two separate tensors again. During this SVD, a truncation is
typically unavoidable, leading to a measurable truncation error.
Careful analysis of this truncation error is necessary as always, but
also proceeds in much the same way as always. In 1TDVP, this error is
exactly zero.

The fourth source of error lies in the inexact solution of the local
equations. Using sufficiently many Krylov vectors locally, it is very
easy to make this error small. Therefore, one should always use
sufficiently many vectors such that the obtained error is at least
smaller than the truncation error in the previous step.

Note that changing the time-step size
\delta
in the TDVP affects the
four errors differently: the projection and truncation error affect
each time step relatively independently of the size of that step.
Hence, increasing the number of time steps during a fixed total time
evolution increases the projection and truncation errors. The finite
time-step error and the error from the inexact local solution, on the
other hand, decrease when increasing the number of time steps and the
total time is kept fixed. As such, choosing a smaller
\delta

decreases the time-step error but increases the projection and
truncation error. It is hence typically useful to take some care when
choosing, e.g., the truncation threshold and the time-step size such as
to approximately balance the induced errors.

Additionally, the energy and norm of the state are conserved exactly
within the 1TDVP and only affected by the truncation error in the
2TDVP. This exact conservation may extend to those quantities which
are contained within the Hamiltonian
[
85
,
84
]
.
While such energy conservation is certainly very helpful to obtain
long-time hydrodynamic observables such as diffusion constants, care
has to be taken when using only 1TDVP during the calculation as shown
in Ref.
[
87
]
. Specifically, one has to take great care
to ensure that the obtained data is completely converged in the bond
dimension of the state at all times.

1:

procedure

Sweep-Right
(
\delta
,

,
\{R_{j}\}_{j=3}^{L+1}
,

,

)

for

do

3:

4:

T^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j,j+1}\leftarrow\mathrm{exp}(-\mathrm{i}\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j,j+1)})T^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j,j+1}
using
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j,j+1)}\equiv L^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1}\cdot W^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j}\cdot W^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+1}\cdot R^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+2}

6:

M_{j+1}\leftarrow C_{\underline{j}}\cdot B_{j+1}

7:

if

j\neq L-1

then

8:

L_{j}\leftarrow\textsc{Contract-Left}(L_{j-1},W_{j},A_{j})

9:

M^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+1}\leftarrow\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{i}\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j+1})M^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+1}
using
\hat{H}_{j+1}^{\mathrm{eff}}\equiv L^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j}\cdot W^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+1}\cdot R^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+2}

10:

Delete
R_{j+2}

11:

end

if

12:

end

for

13:

return

\{L_{j}\}_{j=0}^{L-2}
,
\{A_{j},M_{L}\}_{j=1}^{L-1}

14:

end

procedure

15:

procedure

Sweep-Left
(
\delta
,
R_{L+1}
,
\{L_{j}\}_{j=0}^{L-2}
,
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{L}
,
\{A_{j},M_{L}\}_{j=1}^{L-1}
)

16:

for

j\in[L,2]

do

17:

T_{j-1,j}\leftarrow\sum_{m_{j-1}}A_{j-1;m_{j-2},m_{j-1}}^{\sigma_{j-1}}M_{j;m_{j-1},m_{i}}^{\sigma_{i}}

18:

T^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1,j}\leftarrow\mathrm{exp}(-\mathrm{i}\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j-1,j)})T^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1,j}
using
\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{(j-1,j)}\equiv L^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-2}\cdot W^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1}\cdot W^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j}\cdot R^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j+1}

19:

A_{j-1},C_{\underline{j-1}},B_{j}\leftarrow T_{j-1,j}
via singular value decomposition and truncation

20:

M_{j-1}\leftarrow A_{j-1}\cdot C_{\underline{j-1}}

21:

if

j\neq 2

then

22:

R_{j}\leftarrow\textsc{Contract-Right}(R_{j+1},W_{j},B_{j})

23:

M^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1}\leftarrow\mathrm{exp}(\mathrm{i}\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}\hat{H}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{j-1})M^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1}
using
\hat{H}_{j-1}^{\mathrm{eff}}\equiv L^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-2}\cdot W^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j-1}\cdot R^{\vphantom{\mathrm{eff}}}_{j}

24:

Delete
L_{j-2}

25:

end

if

26:

end

for

27:

return

\{R_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L+1}
,
\{M_{1},B_{j}\}_{j=2}^{L}

end

procedure

6.2.3
Algorithm

In practice, the 1/2TDVP method is quite similar to the 1/2DMRG method
without subspace expansion or density matrix perturbation and nearly
identical to the local Krylov method. Compared to the DMRG method, one
of course has to replace the local eigensolver by a local
exponentiation. Compared to both the local Krylov and the DMRG
methods, we also need an additional backwards evolution step either on
each bond (1TDVP) or the second site of a two-site evolution
(2TDVP). This replacement of the ad-hoc basis transformation done by
the local Krylov method with a properly motivated backwards evolution
will result in smaller errors in each step.

The 1TDVP algorithm is described in detail in
Algorithm

5
,
the 2TDVP algorithm in
Algorithm

6
.

7
Additional tricks

Having reviewed the most commonly used methods in detail, we will now
collect and summarize some generic improvements without any claim to
completeness. These are essentially tricks which are relatively
independent of the actual time-evolution method and can mostly be
implemented in all of the methods we just reviewed. They are not
strictly necessary to implement in conjunction with any method and as
such will not be benchmarked in detail later, but they are useful to
keep in mind in case of particularly hard or challenging problems.

7.1
Combining Heisenberg
and Schrödinger picture time evolution

In the context of MPS methods, combining Schrödinger and Heisenberg
picture
[
88
,
89
]
time evolution was first proposed in
Ref.
[
90
]
. Considering a time-dependent observable

\langle\phi|\hat{O}(t)|\psi\rangle

(199)

between two arbitrary states, in the Schrödinger picture we would evaluate

\langle\phi|\hat{O}(t)|\psi\rangle=\left(\langle\phi|e^{\mathrm{i}t\hat{H}}\right)\hat{O}\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}t\hat{H}}|\psi\rangle\right)\quad.

(200)

That is, we apply time-evolution operators to the states

|\phi\rangle
and
|\psi\rangle
to obtain time-evolved states

|\phi(t)\rangle
and
|\psi(t)\rangle
and then evaluate the
time-independent observable between them. The maximum time
t

obtainable is then typically limited by the entanglement growth in the
states, resulting in larger and larger bond dimensions or errors.

In comparison, the Heisenberg picture would see us time evolve the
operator
\hat{O}
as

\langle\phi|\hat{O}(t)|\psi\rangle=\langle\phi|\left(e^{\mathrm{i}t\hat{H}}\hat{O}e^{-\mathrm{i}t\hat{H}}\right)|\psi\rangle

(201)

while keeping the states
|\phi\rangle
,
|\psi\rangle
static. Again,
the maximal obtainable time t is limited by the entanglement growth in
the operator
\hat{O}
and the maximal bond dimension we can use to
represent it.
11
11

11

Note that it is difficult to compare errors
between MPS and MPO truncations, as the error of the MPO truncation
is given by the operator norm whereas during an MPO compression, we
only control the 2-norm of the operator
[
67
]
.
If we now combine the two
evolutions as

\langle\phi|\hat{O}(t_{1}+t_{2})|\psi\rangle=\left(\langle\phi|e^{\mathrm{i}t_{1}\hat{H}}\right)\left(e^{\mathrm{i}t_{2}\hat{H}}\hat{O}e^{-\mathrm{i}t_{2}\hat{H}}\right)\left(e^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\hat{H}}|\psi\rangle\right)

(202)

we can obtain times
t_{1}+t_{2}
while only requiring MPO and MPS bond
dimensions typical of times
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
respectively. Note that in
this case, the computationally limiting operation is no longer the
time evolution itself but the evaluation of observables given as the
tensor network of a large-
w
MPO between two large-
m

MPS
[
90
]
.

7.2
Complex time steps

Let us assume that we have a time-evolution operator

\hat{U}^{\prime}(\delta)=\mathbf{\hat{1}}-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}
which is exact
to first order. Applying this operator to a state will result in an
error
O(\delta^{2})
compared to the exact evolution with the operator

\hat{U}(\delta)=e^{-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}}
. Repeating the
process
T/\delta
times to obtain a state at final time
T
, we
incur an error
O(\delta^{2})\nicefrac{{T}}{{\delta}}=O(\delta)
.
However, if we allow complex intermediate steps
\delta_{1}
and

\delta_{2}
, we can solve

\hat{U}^{\prime}(\delta_{1})\hat{U}^{\prime}(\delta_{2})=\mathbf{\hat{1}}-\mathrm{i}\delta\hat{H}-\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}\hat{H}^{2}

(203)

for
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
by expanding the left-hand side:

\displaystyle\quad\hat{U}^{\prime}(\delta_{1})\hat{U}^{\prime}(\delta_{2})

(204)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\left(\mathbf{\hat{1}}-\mathrm{i}\delta_{1}\hat{H}\right)\left(\mathbf{\hat{1}}-\mathrm{i}\delta_{2}\hat{H}\right)

(205)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{\hat{1}}-\mathrm{i}(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2})\hat{H}-\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\hat{H}^{2}

(206)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}=\delta\quad\land\quad\delta_{1}\delta_{2}=\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}\;.

(207)

Two solutions are possible, one of them is

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\frac{1-\mathrm{i}}{2}\delta\quad\land\quad\delta_{2}=\frac{1+\mathrm{i}}{2}\delta\quad.

(208)

Choosing these values for
\delta_{1,2}
then results in a third-order
error per time step and a second-order error overall. This choice of time steps is
suggested in particular in combination with the MPO
W^{\mathrm{I,II}}
method to obtain a better error per time step. The cost of the
method only grows linearly with the number of evolution operators and,
e.g., four operators
\hat{U}^{\prime}(\delta_{1,2,3,4})
are required for
a third-order error
[
40
]
.

The drawback is the loss of unitarity at each individual time step
which may be disadvantageous. Furthermore, if the time evolution is
purely imaginary (e.g., for finite-temperature calculations) and the
Hamiltonian does not contain complex coefficients, one may avoid
complex arithmetic entirely and only use real floating-point scalars
for 50% less memory usage and an approximately four-fold speed-up on
matrix multiplications. Unfortunately, it is then impossible to use this
trick to reduce the time-step error.

7.3
Green’s functions 1: Removal of low-lying states

This trick was first proposed in Ref.
[
31
]

and is relatively straightforward to implement. Assume a ground state

|0\rangle
as an MPS obtained via DMRG and let us shift the
Hamiltonian such that this state has energy
E_{0}=0
,

\hat{H}|0\rangle=0
. We are then interested in the time-dependent
observable

x(t)=\langle 0|\hat{A}(t)\hat{B}|0\rangle

(209)

where
\hat{A}
and
\hat{B}
are typically local operators such as
creators or annihilators. The evolution of
\hat{B}|0\rangle
is
generically non-trivial and if we want to capture all frequency
contributions in
x(t)
, we need to evolve until at least times

t^{\prime}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{E_{1}}}
where
E_{1}
is the energy of the lowest
eigenstate with non-zero energy
|1\rangle
contained in

\hat{B}|0\rangle
. In contrast, to capture contributions of
higher-energy states
|n\rangle
with energies
E_{n}>E_{1}
, we only
need to evolve to shorter times

t^{\prime\prime}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{E_{n}}}<t^{\prime}
.

However, a few low-lying eigenstates can often be calculated also with
DMRG by orthogonalizing against previously-found eigenstates. Hence if
we run DMRG multiple times, we can obtain not just the ground state

|0\rangle
but also further eigenstates
|1\rangle
,
|2\rangle

etc. If we use quantum numbers and
\hat{B}
changes the quantum number
of the state, these additional eigenstates should be calculated in the
quantum number sector of
\hat{B}|0\rangle
. If we then orthogonalize

\hat{B}|0\rangle
against
|1\rangle
,
|2\rangle
etc., we remove
the contributions which rotate (in real-time) or decay (in
imaginary-time evolutions) the slowest and hence require the longest
time evolutions. The evolution of the removed states can then be done
exactly as we know both their energy and initial weight in

\hat{B}|0\rangle
. Even missing one of the eigenstates due to
convergence problems with DMRG does not introduce an error but merely
decreases the effectivity of the method.

7.4
Green’s functions 2: Linear prediction

Calculating dynamical structure factors or more generally spectral
functions from time-dependent data requires two Fourier
transformations: first, one needs to transform from real-space to
momentum-space and second from real-time to frequency. The former
transformation is typically unproblematic, but the latter
transformation suffers either from overdamping or strong spectral
leakage if the available maximal time
t
is insufficient. Linear
prediction
[
91
,
92
,
93
]
assumes that the real-time momentum-space
Green’s function
G(k,t)
is composed of multiple distinct exponentially
decaying and oscillating contributions arising from a distinct pole
structure of
G(k,\omega)
. If this is true for a time series
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
,
an additional data point
\tilde{x}_{n}
can be approximated well by the
form

\tilde{x}_{n}=-\sum_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}x_{n-i-1}

(210)

with suitably chosen coefficients
a_{i}
independent of
n
. We hence
first compute a finite time series which is still as long as we can
manage with time-dependent MPS methods. Subsequently, we need to find
coefficients
a_{i}
such that the above holds for the data we computed
exactly. Using those
a_{i}
, we can then extend the time series to
arbitrarily long times to generate a sufficiently long time series
that a subsequent Fourier transform only requires minimal damping and
hence provides for clear features.

It is useful to divide the available calculated data into three
segments: first, one should discard an interval
[x_{0},\ldots,x_{D-1}]

at the beginning which captures short-time physics irrelevant and
untypical of the longer-time behavior. Second, a fitting interval

[x_{D},\ldots,x_{D+N-1}]
of N points is selected over which the
coefficients
a_{i}
are minimized. Third, trust in the prediction is
increased if it coincides with additional calculated data

[x_{D+N},\ldots,x_{\mathrm{max}}]
outside the fitting interval.

To select the
a_{i}
, we want to minimize the error

\epsilon=\sum_{k=D}^{D+N-1}|\tilde{x}_{k}-x_{k}|^{2}\quad.

(211)

Note that to evaluate
\tilde{x}_{k}
,
D
must be larger than the
number of coefficients
p
. The coefficient vector
\underline{a}
is obtained as

\underline{a}=-\underline{R}^{-1}\underline{r}

(212)

where the matrix
\underline{R}
and vector
\underline{r}
have entries

\displaystyle R_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=D}^{D+N-1}x_{k-i}^{\star}x_{k-j}

(213)

\displaystyle r_{i}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=D}^{D+N-1}x_{k-i}^{\star}x_{k}

(214)

respectively. Once the
a_{i}
are obtained, data ideally can be
generated initially for the interval

[x_{D+N},\ldots,x_{\mathrm{max}}]
and, once verified to coincide
with the calculated data, extended to arbitrary times.

Several numerical pitfalls need to be considered here: First, the
matrix
\underline{R}
may be singular. Two possible remedies include
addition of a small shift
\varepsilon
or reduction of the number of
parameters
p
. Ideally the latter should be considered, but may lead
to problems finding the optimal non-singular
p
. Second, if we
construct the vector

\underline{x}_{n}=[x_{n-1},\ldots,x_{n-p}]^{T}

(215)

we can move it forward one step as

\underline{\tilde{x}}_{n+1}=\underline{A}{\hskip 2.84544pt}\underline{x}_{n}

(216)

where the matrix
\underline{A}
is of the form

\underline{A}=\begin{pmatrix}-a_{1}&-a_{2}&-a_{3}&\cdots&-a_{p}\\
1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&1&0\end{pmatrix}

(217)

and its eigenvalues
\alpha_{i}
contain the frequencies and dampings of
the aforementioned oscillations and exponential decays. As such,

\alpha_{i}>1
are unphysical and need to be dealt with, it appears

[
93
]
that setting those contributions to zero works
best.

7.5
Purification insertion point (PIP)

Figure 19
:
Shifting the purification insertion point to reduce computational complexity of the time evolution in OTOCs.

Calculating out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOC) allows us to
measure the scrambling of quantum information and finds many
interesting and current applications. In general an OTOC of operators

\hat{W},\hat{V}
is given as an ensemble average

\displaystyle C^{\hat{V},\hat{W}}_{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left\{\hat{\rho}(\beta)\left[\hat{V}(t),\hat{W}\right]^{\dagger}\left[\hat{V}(t),\hat{W}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Real\left[\Tr\left\{\hat{\rho}(\beta)\hat{V}^{\dagger}(t)\hat{W}^{\dagger}\hat{V}(t)\hat{W}\right\}\right]}_{\equiv F^{\hat{V},\hat{W}}_{\beta}(t)}+\text{time ordered}

(218)

wherein we have suppressed the time-ordered terms and define the OTOC as the out-of-time ordered part

F^{\hat{V},\hat{W}}_{\beta}(t)
. At finite temperature, we have to use a
purification to evaluate this quantity. If we would calculate the
time evolutions in
F^{\hat{V},\hat{W}}_{\beta}(t)
naively by direct evolution
only in the physical degrees of freedom we would require

\mathcal{O}(N^{2})
time steps to obtain the OTOC at time

t=N\delta
.
Clearly, the growing numerical expenses forbid to reach both large
system sizes and long time scales
t
. The graphical notion (see

Fig.

19
) immediately suggests to transform the operators
in the OTOC in some way as to evenly distribute the required time
evolutions leading to only linear scaling of effort in time
t
. In
the following, we will explain how to transform these operators in the
purification picture and alter the purification insertion point
(PIP). For related work in the framework of matrix-product operators,
cf. Ref.
[
94
]
.

Consider the ensemble average

F_{\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z},\beta}\equiv\Tr\left\{\hat{\rho}(\beta)\hat{Z}\hat{Y}\hat{X}\right\}
for some global
operators
\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z}
at inverse temperature
\beta
.
Using the cyclic property of the trace the ensemble average can now be written as expectation value in the enlarged Hilbert space

F_{\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z},\beta}=\Tr\left\{\hat{\rho}(\beta)\hat{Z}\hat{Y}\hat{X}\right\}=\bra{0}\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{Z}\hat{Y}\hat{X}\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\ket{0}\equiv\bra{\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}}}\hat{Z}\hat{Y}\hat{X}\ket{\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}}}\;,

(219)

where we have introduced the purified finite temperature state
\ket{\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}}}\equiv\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\ket{0}
based on the infinite temperature state
\ket{0}
(cf.
Section

2.7
).
A graphical representation of recasting the trace into an expectation value is given by the two networks
Fig.

20(a)
and
Fig.

20(b)
with out-going indices representing row vectors and in-going indices column vectors.

(a)

Ensemble average as trace

(b)

Canonically purified

ensemble average

(c)

Ensemble average with shifted PIP

Figure 20
:
Different choices of operator purifications for ensemble average
F_{\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z},\beta}=\Tr\left\{\hat{\rho}(\beta)\hat{Z}\hat{Y}\hat{X}\right\}
.

Figure 21
:

Local basis optimization matrices
U_{j}
are
inserted on the physical legs of the MPS to transform a large
physical basis (indicated by doubled lines) into a smaller
effective basis of the MPS tensors
M_{j}
.

From the pictographical representation we motivate the infinite temperature state
\ket{0}
to be represented by a rank
(2,0)
tensor
\ket{0}\equiv\sum_{a,\bar{b}}D^{a,\bar{b}}\ket{a}\ket{\bar{b}}
and correspondingly
\bra{0}
by a rank
(0,2)
tensor
\bra{0}\equiv\sum_{a,\bar{b}}D_{a,\bar{b}}\bra{a}\bra{\bar{b}}
, where we have placed a bar over those indices labeling ancilla degrees of freedom.

These tensors have to fulfill the orthogonality conditions

\sum_{\bar{b}}D^{a,\bar{b}}D_{c,\bar{b}}=\delta^{a}_{c},\quad\sum_{a}D^{a,\bar{b}}D_{a,\bar{c}}=\delta^{\bar{b}}_{\bar{c}}

(220)

so that the tensor elements can be choosen to be
D^{a,\bar{b}}\equiv D^{a,\bar{b}}\delta^{\bar{a}}_{\bar{b}}
and
D_{a,\bar{b}}\equiv D_{a,\bar{b}}\delta^{\bar{b}}_{\bar{a}}
.
When contracted over physical degrees of freedom, the action of these tensors is to convert row vectors into column vectors and vice versa

D\hat{O}D^{\dagger}=\sum_{a,c}\sum_{\bar{b},\bar{d}}D^{a,\bar{b}}\hat{O}^{c}_{a}D_{c,\bar{d}}\ket{\bar{b}}\bra{\bar{d}}=\sum_{\bar{b},\bar{d}}\hat{O}^{\bar{a}}_{\bar{c}}\ket{\bar{a}}\bra{\bar{c}}=\hat{O}^{t}\;.

(221)

If we now interpret indices carrying a bar as maps between ancilla degrees of freedom we can reformulate the purification in terms of the
D
tensors

F_{\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z},\beta}=\sum_{a,c,\ldots,g,\bar{b}}D_{a,\bar{b}}\hat{\rho}^{a}_{c}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{Z}^{c}_{d}\hat{Y}^{d}_{e}\hat{X}^{e}_{f}\hat{\rho}^{f}_{g}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})D^{g,\bar{b}}\;.

(222)

Inserting identities on the physical Hilbert space between
\hat{\rho}
and
\hat{X}
as well as
\hat{X}
and
\hat{Y}
and making explicit use of the representation of
\hat{D}
we obtain

\displaystyle F_{\hat{X},\hat{Y},\hat{Z},\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,c,d,g,\bar{b},\\
\bar{e},\bar{f},e_{l},e_{r},f_{l},f_{r}\end{subarray}}D_{a,\bar{b}}\rho^{a}_{c}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{Z}^{c}_{d}\hat{Y}^{d}_{e_{l}}\underbrace{D^{e_{l},\bar{e}}D_{e_{r},\bar{e}}}_{\delta^{e_{l}}_{e_{r}}}\hat{X}^{e_{r}}_{f_{l}}\underbrace{D^{f_{l},\bar{f}}D_{f_{r},\bar{f}}}_{\delta^{f_{l}}_{f_{r}}}\rho^{f_{r}}_{g}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})D^{g,\bar{b}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,c,d,\bar{b},\\
\bar{e},\bar{f},e_{l}\end{subarray}}D_{a,\bar{b}}\rho^{a}_{c}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{Z}^{c}_{d}\hat{Y}^{d}_{e_{l}}D^{e_{l},\bar{e}}\underbrace{\hat{X}^{\bar{f}}_{\bar{e}}\rho^{\bar{b}}_{\bar{f}}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})}_{\text{act on $\mathcal{H}_{A}$}}

(223)

\displaystyle=\bra{0}\left(\hat{\rho}^{t}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{X}^{t}\right)_{A}\otimes\left(\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{Z}\hat{Y}\right)_{P}\ket{0}

(224)

so that now
\sum_{\bar{f}}\hat{X}^{\bar{f}}_{\bar{e}}\hat{\rho}^{\bar{b}}_{\bar{f}}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\equiv\hat{\rho}^{t}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{X}^{t}
are acting on the ancilla space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, i.e., we have shifted the purification insertion point.
Again these manipulations can be represented efficiently in a graphical notation and are given in
Fig.

20(c)
.

Using this procedure, we can rewrite the OTOC
F^{\hat{V},\hat{W}}_{\beta}(t)
as

\displaystyle F^{\hat{V},\hat{W}}_{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=\Real\left[\Tr\left\{\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{U}(t)\hat{W}^{\dagger}\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\hat{V}\hat{U}(t)\hat{W}\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\Real\left[\bra{0}\left(\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\hat{V}\hat{U}(t)\hat{W}\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\right)^{t}_{A}\otimes\left(\hat{\rho}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t)\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{U}(t)\hat{W}^{\dagger}\right)_{P}\ket{0}\right]\;.

(225)

Defining the initial states

\displaystyle\ket{W}

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{W}^{\dagger}_{P}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{1}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}_{A}\ket{0}\;,

(226)

\displaystyle\ket{W_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{\rho}_{P}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\otimes\left(\hat{W}^{*}\hat{\rho}_{A}(\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{2}})\right)\ket{0}\;,

(227)

and their purified time evolutions

\displaystyle\ket{W(t)}
